## logistics

*2022-05-24 to 2022-06-28 · 24 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/logistics/with/1384125)*

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-05-24 19:14 UTC

Hi @everyone !

I created this stream for managing the redesign project, and making sure we can help each other keep the work moving forward. In order to do that, I'd like us to post regular updates here on how things are going with the redesign issues are working on, and especially if there are questions, things that feel stuck, etc.

To accommodate different schedules, I would propose trying something like:

1. Everyone posts updates a couple of times a week, e.g. by 10 AM Pacific on Tuesdays and Fridays.
2. It's not required, but folks generally try to be around at those times to talk through things live.

Does that sounds reasonable/helpful? Any other requests, suggestions, etc.?

Whatever we decide, we should definitely be super open to changing it up if we try it for a couple of weeks and find that something is not working well.

If it sounds good overall, we can do a discussion/poll to try to find some good times for updates/(optional) chats.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-14 19:27 UTC

@Aman (amanagr) is probably the right person to help with this. One thing worth thinking about is to what extent we want to try to structure the CSS for this page rebuild in a way such that it shares almost no CSS with the existing landing pages codebase; that would be helpful if our goal is to delete all or parts of the existing `landing-page.css` at the end of a sequence of reworking all the individual pages.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-14 19:32 UTC

I think the high level structure, though, is that:
* `static/styles/portico/` contains all the files for pages that are not the app.
* `static/styles/portico/portico.css` has shared CSS between landing pages and app-adjacent pages like login/signup and the help center.
* `static/styles/landing_page.css` has CSS that's only used in the landing pages.
* The stuff specific to individual pages is in blocks like this: `.portico-landing.features-app`
* There's also Bootstrap and a bunch of CSS reset stuff like `p` and `a` styling in those pages.

In an ideal world, I think we'd do rewritten landing pages like this with `boostrap` and that CSS reset stuff not included at all, so that the work we're doing here is also on a path to deletion of bootstrap from the landing pages codebase.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-14 19:34 UTC

I'm not sure how realistic that plan is, but if we want to try to do that, we probably want to create a new directory with perhaps a similar file structure (at least in terms of having a structure that supports sharing in the ways we want) that has versions of those files that are post-redesign.

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-11-14 19:34 UTC

We decided to go with bootstrap 5 last time as I see it in the PR #16326. Do we still want to do that here?

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-11-14 19:42 UTC

(Personally, I don't think we should use bootstrap here)

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-11-14 19:43 UTC

(replying to Tim Abbott)
> In an ideal world, I think we'd do rewritten landing pages like this with `boostrap` and that CSS reset stuff not included at all, so that the work we're doing here is also on a path to deletion of bootstrap from the landing pages codebase.

Did you mean without?

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-11-14 19:45 UTC

> Did you mean without?

I think that was a "with... not included", i.e. without. :)

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-14 20:45 UTC

I think we should avoid using Bootstrap if we can for the landing pages.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-11-15 13:02 UTC

I’m using completely new styles and no dependencies for a new page

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-11-15 13:15 UTC

In our last meeting regarding the new landing page which I was a part of (maybe a year ago), we decided to separate the new landing page from the main codebase (zulip/zulip) and use something like [`11ty`](https://www.11ty.dev/) to generate the website. https://github.com/amanagr/eleventy-netlify-boilerplate is close to how it would look like if we decide to go in that direction and possibly save me a lot of time.

Where do we stand on that right now?

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-15 19:06 UTC

Well we've done some work towards decoupling -- for example, moving most existing landing pages content to the `corporate` app. There's still various complex tangles that we'd need to resolve in order to fully decouple -- stuff like /integrations.

I think my main thought is that the homepage redesign is a pretty important improvement to our marketing site, and I don't want us to block on it on first doing a complex other migration; I'd rather have resources that might go towards such a migration work on implementing our redesign.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-11-16 03:49 UTC

sorry but main page isn't a typical markdown content driven page. I don't know how exactly content is managed but for now suggest to release things as html code

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-16 05:43 UTC

Yeah agreed it's definitely going to need to be an HTML page.

## Storybook

*2022-06-04 to 2023-01-11 · 26 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/Storybook/with/1388530)*

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-04 15:06 UTC

It is a project management question. Earlier we discussed the desire to have https://storybook.js.org/ so we could work on styling/components separately from the integration. Do you think it is a time to make it happen?

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-04 15:08 UTC

I don't know if you guys decided on any framework but Storybook is useful with just pure html css too https://storybook.js.org/docs/html/get-started/introduction

**Rohitt Vashishtha** · 2022-06-04 18:01 UTC

I like this website. It looks like codepen but without having to do a lot of setup first.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2022-06-04 23:27 UTC

Our current plan of charging boldly sideways will not allow us to make forward progress on the deep architectural issues that make it impossible to extract reusable components that could be viewed in Storybook from our tangled soup of CSS and JavaScript.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-04 23:29 UTC

Sorry, I didn't understand what are you trying to say.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2022-06-04 23:34 UTC

Storybook is a tool for visualizing isolated UI components. We don’t have any of those. We can’t as long as our CSS is permanently infested with Bootstrap and fragile cascading structures, the bulk of our JS is a gigantic import cycle hundreds of files big, and our state is all flying around in global variables.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2022-06-04 23:48 UTC

I know that every time I explain why we need to do a major rewrite with a sane architecture, that part of the discussion ends up getting split out to a new topic, so we can get back to throwing more mud at our pile of mud. I get it. It’s the path of least resistance and it isn’t anyone’s fault. But if we ever want to have nice things, more of us are going to need to start paying some attention to these architectural problems.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-05 00:10 UTC

Oh, I see. I thought that "redesign" is a good reason to stop doing piles and start doing things the right way. I thought that having "components" in Storybook even if they are just css based html tags would push such initiative forward. 

I very support sane architecture. Should we initiate another discussion about that to understand when and how it should be done?

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2022-06-05 00:16 UTC

Yeah I thought so too, but observationally, what we seem to have been doing is charging ahead with the redesign piece by piece under the old architecture…

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-05 00:27 UTC

I don't see any redesign task is actually started except the search input on the top. I think this is the best time to rethink the approach and at least make some intermediate change to enable future better architecture.

THe redesign is huge initiative and if it will be "dirty done" future changes would be very hard to support.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-05 00:27 UTC

@Tim Abbott it looks like we should talk about it.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-05 00:29 UTC

I'm ready to be a developer force on the side of "components" even if we choose no framework step for now.

**Sayam Samal** · 2022-06-05 07:14 UTC

> We can’t as long as our CSS is permanently infested with Bootstrap and fragile cascading structures
@Anders Kaseorg I actually plan on working on the CSS architecture as part of my [GSOC '22 proposal](https://docs.google.com/document/d/1rVi1waPQSGopnCpf06W7DYDmwGj5BkFQL660viot64s/edit?usp=sharing). I have going through https://cssguidelin.es/, posts on css-tricks and bem-naming conventions and looking at examples of those knowledgeable. I plan to work on selector specificity, portability and re-usability. Since I already plan on working on the redesign area the most, I think I'll have opportunities to tackle these as I work on the redesign tasks one by one.

**Sayam Samal** · 2022-06-05 07:15 UTC

I plan on starting a topic on this soon where I'll like to share my research and ideas. Which would be a good stream for that?

**Sayam Samal** · 2022-06-05 07:19 UTC

> I thought that "redesign" is a good reason to stop doing piles and start doing things the right way.
@Vlad Korobov  I definitely agree.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-06 19:14 UTC

I agree with Anders that we need to focus on CSS architecture issues ... probably most important of which is removing Bootstrap. We can continue making progress on design changes while doing so -- for a lot of the redesign work we've started implementation on, 80% of the work is in determining what we want the UX to be, not the CSS implementation.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-06 19:15 UTC

In other words, we're trying to focus on just those changes that are orthogonal to these CSS architecture issues. Which is helpful for making some progress, but it's not going to get to a good result if we're working on the CSS architecture stuff in parallel.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-06 19:16 UTC

What I think we need is to put together a concrete plan for how we're going to get Bootstrap out of the system. And possibly also concrete plans for some of the other things that hold back our ability to design components independently, but that's really the main one, because Bootstrap prevents ANY component from being independent.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-06 19:19 UTC

#**frontend>bootstrap** has some background around what we've done towards reducing the complexity of Bootstrap removal last summer.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-07 04:30 UTC

> Bootstrap prevents ANY component from being independent

Can we avoid using bootstrap just in the new components? Or there are set of default styles for html tags?

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2022-06-07 05:34 UTC

Right, Bootstrap comes with a highly invasive set of global default styles. It’s written for programmers who want to quickly launch an app with whatever look is fashionable at the time without writing CSS, not for designers who want control over details or for projects with more than a short-term maintenance horizon. What Zulip calls CSS is more accurately thought of as a delta against Bootstrap. There’s no way to avoid it selectively.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-07 06:10 UTC

There are probably ways involving CSS compilation to avoid it selectively... but they're probably mostly insane options.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-07 11:51 UTC

Ok, so what is the plan to remove bootstrap globals?

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-08 23:53 UTC

I'll start writing one up in #**frontend>bootstrap**. In short, we probably shouldn't count on it finishing in the next 3 months ... but we may be able to tactically remove specified Bootstrap chunks more easily. 

I don't know what this means for whether we can usefully use a tool like Storybook.

**Daniil Fadeev** · 2023-01-11 18:07 UTC

Hey everyone! I stumbled upon this topic while trying to find information on the components we're using or any docs about this topic. How are things going with Bootstrap and Storybook currently?

**Tim Abbott** · 2023-01-11 21:38 UTC

Progress on removing bootstrap continues, we're down to about 1900 lines of Bootstrap that we haven't deleted. More in #**frontend>bootstrap CSS reset removal**.

## font size

*2022-06-08 to 2023-03-17 · 37 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/font.20size/with/1390526)*

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-08 21:39 UTC

So one of the things that's going to be a complication for a lot of the larger redesign issues like #22022 is that the designs and details include a change in default font size from 14px to 15px. 

I wanted to brainstorm strategies for how to manage that. My guess, not having tested it, is that if we were to change the font size for just the message feed from 14px to 15px, and not, say, the sidebars, that the contrast between the two sizes would look visually off.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-08 21:43 UTC

Which sorta leaves us with a handful of options:
* We could decide we're OK with that sort of visual mismatch existing for a period of a month for more in the name of progress.
* We could figure out some sort of plan for how to write the CSS for redesigned components in using a 15px size as specced, and then scale it by 14/15, in a way that we could hopefully just delete the scaling when we change the default font size. Perhaps using some variables in the CSS files or something?
* We could try to get PRs for each of the components ready but not merge any of them until they'll all ready. I kinda hate this idea because one basically never wants to make 7 projects all need to finish at the same time :).
* We could increase the font size for Zulip as a whole to 15px before starting serious work on any of those PRs. @Anders Kaseorg and I did some early work on this last year and I think it would be possible to do this with a moderate level of visual regressions... which we could probably address in a whack-a-mole fashion if we felt it essential to unblock doing these UI changes.

**Greg Price** · 2022-06-08 23:26 UTC

(replying to Tim Abbott)
> Which sorta leaves us with a handful of options:
>
> * We could decide we're OK with that sort of visual mismatch existing for a period of a month for more in the name of progress.

I'd be wary of this plan because it seems like it would leave us in a state we're not comfortable releasing -- with potentially an unbounded amount of work needed before it'd be back to a releasable state.

That is, it has a lot in common with this plan:

> * We could try to get PRs for each of the components ready but not merge any of them until they'll all ready. I kinda hate this idea because one basically never wants to make 7 projects all need to finish at the same time :).

**Greg Price** · 2022-06-08 23:28 UTC

> * We could figure out some sort of plan for how to write the CSS for redesigned components in using a 15px size as specced, and then scale it by 14/15, in a way that we could hopefully just delete the scaling when we change the default font size. Perhaps using some variables in the CSS files or some …

This sounds appealing if there's some way to do it that (a) doesn't make the code too complicated, (b) means the redesigned components will look acceptably good even while they're being scaled by 14/15. Not sure how plausible or implausible that is.

**Greg Price** · 2022-06-08 23:29 UTC

> * We could increase the font size for Zulip as a whole to 15px before starting serious work on any of those PRs. @Anders Kaseorg and I did some early work on this last year and I think it would be possible to do this with a moderate level of visual regressions... which we could probably address in a whack-a-mole fashion if we felt it essential to unblock doing these UI changes.

Given how long we've said we want to increase the default font size, this sounds pretty good if we can pull it off.

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-06-08 23:41 UTC

> I'd be wary of this plan because it seems like it would leave us in a state we're not comfortable releasing -- with potentially an unbounded amount of work needed before it'd be back to a releasable state.

I think if we go this route, we should explicitly have a plan for how to not block a release on resolving this state, e.g. deciding that we're fine releasing it as-is.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-06-09 00:15 UTC

Yeah, I think that option would be "we test and determine it doesn't look terrible to have the message feed have 1px bigger font than the sidebars."

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-06-09 04:24 UTC

Hey 

>We could decide we're OK with that sort of visual mismatch existing for a period of a month for more in the name of progress.

doesn't mean   "not comfortable releasing"

First of all I didn't think that the feed would be the first thing to redesign, I thought we are going top bar > side bar > another side bar> compose box> feed

**Greg Price** · 2022-06-09 04:32 UTC

Yeah, if we decide we're OK with the mismatch, including with potentially making a release with it, then great.

That does mean being OK with it for a period quite a lot longer than a month. On our current plan for a new faster release cycle, releases of Zulip Server are 4 months apart -- which means anything we release will be what most non-Zulip-Cloud users of Zulip are experiencing for at least 4 months.

We should do a PR to just add most of the new colors we want to use in the codebase as variables so that they are easy to use across  PRs and will make the code review much easier for others. We can then test slightly changing these color values easily too. Thoughts?

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-08-02 12:29 UTC

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-08-02 12:49 UTC

**Evy** · 2022-08-02 19:40 UTC

I would LOVE for us to use more css vars, great suggestion

**Vlad Korobov** · 2022-08-02 20:12 UTC

@Anders Kaseorg what do you think about it?

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2022-08-03 00:23 UTC

There was some previous discussion at #**frontend>css color vars** and #**frontend>variables in scss**.

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-08-03 16:04 UTC

Great! So, we take the [idea](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/stream/6-frontend/topic/variables.20in.20scss/near/1184419) and apply it here for the new colors we want to use. Doing the whole project (for all the colors) now doesn't make sense.

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-08-03 16:04 UTC

```scss
body {
  --color-text: black;
  --color-background: white;
}

body.night-mode {
  --color-text: white;
  --color-background: black;
}

@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) {
    body.color-scheme-automatic {
        @extend body.night-mode;
    }
}

```

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-08-04 14:22 UTC

If #22661 gets merged first, then we can rebase other PRs to follow this --color-variable scheme.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-08-05 00:51 UTC

## Bootstrap removal dependency

*2022-11-21 to 2022-11-21 · 2 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/Bootstrap.20removal.20dependency/with/1467888)*

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-21 03:56 UTC

@Anders Kaseorg and I had a chat about Bootstrap removal on Friday. One observation that we had is that removing 100% of Bootstrap is not necessary to make it easy to add new components with fresh CSS; to do that, we just need to remove the Bootstrap CSS reset (i.e. unscoped CSS like settings for all `<select>` elements).

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-11-21 03:57 UTC

So I did a bit of planning related to that goal, and also a bit of other preparation in #**frontend>bootstrap**, including the creation of this label: https://github.com/zulip/zulip/labels/bootstrap%20removal.


## review process

*2022-12-06 to 2022-12-07 · 16 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/review.20process/with/1474964)*

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-12-06 17:53 UTC

Hi folks! I'd love to brainstorm a bit about how we can make the PR review / iteration process more efficient. A few ideas:

- Process-wise, let's try sequencing code review ahead of  the detailed design review by @Vlad Korobov. Vlad's feedback probably won't change the overall structure of the implementation too much, and this should reduce the number of times that Vlad needs to look at a PR. (I'll still take a look at the PR early on.)

- @Tim Abbott has mentioned that it helps his workflow if any time his review is requested, there is a bit of info as to the stage of the PR / what type of feedback is expected. Some examples might be: addressing questions about the overall approach (the PR is WIP), an initial code review, answering follow-up questions on a code review, reviewing the PR for possible CZO deployment after code review feedback has been addressed, etc.

- As a reminder, because the redesign project is a top priority, I'm happy to prioritize reviewing redesign PRs even when I'm a bit behind on other reviews. Please ping me (e.g., in checkins) if you have a redesign PR ready for me to take a look.

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-12-06 17:54 UTC

Anyhow, would love to get thoughts from other folks (CC @Aman (amanagr) , @Evy ) on where things have been dragging, and how we can make the process go faster / more smoothly. :)

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-12-06 17:54 UTC

@Tim Abbott, did you have some thoughts on PR structure that might help here?

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-12-06 17:59 UTC

**Neil Pilgrim (neiljp)** · 2022-12-06 18:09 UTC

**Aman (amanagr)** · 2022-12-06 18:12 UTC

**Evy** · 2022-12-06 18:42 UTC

I can try adding information about the stage of the PR, but generally have been assuming there isn't bandwidth for looking at WIP PRs (though that would be really helpful for me!) and have been mostly asking for initial code reviews (after product review is finished) and CZO-deployment reviews.

**Evy** · 2022-12-06 18:50 UTC

For the stage of PR --  would having labels on GitHub showing which of those stages it's in be helpful to Tim?

* WIP, seeking feedback
* Initial review
* Continuing conversation in comments
* Followup review
* CZO deployment
* Awaiting merge

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:31 UTC

Generally, technical reviews taking a long time are caused by pull requests that either (1) are hard to understand and verify or (2) have implementation problems that are uncovered in review. The messier a pull request is, the more likely it is to end up implicitly deprioritized by it being hard to find the time to push through integrating it (and there always being more issues even after a reviewer has looked through it 5 times).

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:31 UTC

One of the main ways you can control this as a contributor is to really focus on writing a good series of commits and organizing your work into pull requests that designed to make good use of reviewer time. 
* If you realize we need to rename a module, and do that in the first commit of a commit series, I can merge that commit the first time I review the PR and shrink what needs to be looked at in all subsequent code reviews. Such a PR can move quickly because it requires no design or product review.
* If you create a pull request that's a preparatory refactoring for feature work that cleans up the codebase (say, creates some new abstractions) and is easy to verify the logic behaves similarly to before, we can merge that without any design review -- though we may want brief product review to test that we don't accidentally break something. If the refactoring is thoughtfully done, where one doesn't have important commented logical changes (say, a block with a comment explaining why it's important removed without explanation), these can be reviewed and integrated quickly. These also don't require any chat.zulip.org testing unless they are expected to be particularly disruptive, like the popovers infrastructure rewrite.
* If you have a pull request that only contains CSS changes, it will be primarily reviewed visually both in screenshots and then in chat.zulip.org than if it has a bunch of other random changes. These can usually be safely test-deployed on chat.zulip.org with a very quick skim of a review, because I don't need to think about whether we're going to introduce major logic bugs when deciding when to test-deploy.
* If you create a pull request that does that  simultaneously changes the UI, renames modules, and refactors a bunch of other things, then all of the work in the pull request ends up requiring all of those stages of review.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:32 UTC

The most important thing to understand is that pull request review has significant diseconomies of scale; the more changes you have in a pull request, the more likely it is to get stuck because one of those changes is problematic.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:32 UTC

Further, the more types of things a pull request is changing, the more likely it is to get stuck, because if any of those types of things have a problem, it's not going to be mergeable.

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:37 UTC

Mm, so that sounds like a strong recommendation not only to split changes into appropriate commits, but also to split them into separate PRs when the type of review process required is different.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:38 UTC

Folks who are really good at getting large pull requests merged are very thoughtful about how they break down a project -- one might start with just prototyping the thing to get something vaguely working and feel out the challenges -- but one should be constantly thinking things like:
* "Oh, I should rename this CSS class or function or variable or file because the current name is kinda bad and a lot worse after my changes. Let me pause what I'm doing and do that in a clean, easily merged prep commit."
* "Oh, I really should fix this test suite to not be insane or to exist or whatever in a prep commit, and then my commit that actually changes the behavior can have a 3-line section in the test file to verify it behaves correctly, and that change in the test file will serve to crisply demonstrate that this change is correct to a reviewer". (Obviously only some changes lend themselves to nice tests).
* "Oh, I want to re-architect this JavaScript module to have better abstractions. Let me do that first, finish it, and then come back to the UI change I want to make."

A good way to think about it is that if you're stacking a series of commits to implement a change, you want the lowest risk changes (both technically and in terms of controversy) to be at the start of the branch (or in a prep PR) so that they can be integrated quickly and simplify for everyone what needs to be thought about for further work.

**Alya Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:39 UTC

https://zulip.readthedocs.io/en/latest/contributing/reviewable-prs.html has some of these ideas, but should probably be expanded to incorporate additional notes from this conversation.

**Tim Abbott** · 2022-12-07 20:43 UTC

And then of course if you realize you do need to make a risky change and then stack other work on top of it (even something like picking a name  for a setting that you're going to not want to change later), start a thread explaining the context and your argument for that specific detail explaining your understanding of the trade-offs and your proposed solution on chat.zulip.org; that way we can discuss that and turn it into a low risk change.

**Alya Abbott** · 2023-03-07 18:26 UTC

@Vlad Korobov I realized recently that we never discussed the design for [alert word](https://zulip.com/help/dm-mention-alert-notifications#alert-words) highlighting. I'm guessing you'll want to at least update the colors.

Not a big deal, but we should figure it out and and file an issue (or perhaps add it to #22022).

[Screen-Shot-2023-03-07-at-8.37.49-AM.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/b1/9qj4EwVzKPEtu03_XglFdc96/Screen-Shot-2023-03-07-at-8.37.49-AM.png) [Screen-Shot-2023-03-07-at-8.37.39-AM.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/6/DWF_EOX5YdfVlgPnmhBsMmqU/Screen-Shot-2023-03-07-at-8.37.39-AM.png)

**Tim Abbott** · 2023-03-07 20:39 UTC

The main thing to be careful about is that you can have both alert words and search keyword match highlighting in the same view. E.g. `is:alerted design` will find all the messages containing one of your alert words and the word design; we probably want those two classes of things to look distinct in some sort of intuitive way.

## 7.0 release

*2023-03-29 to 2023-04-11 · 19 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/7.2E0.20release/with/1536013)*

**Alya Abbott** · 2023-03-29 00:02 UTC

We're starting to think about what to aim for in terms of redesign work for the 7.0 release (tentatively scheduled for early May). Here are the major changes @Tim Abbott and I were thinking might be realistic to include. @Aman (amanagr) , @Evy what do you think?

It's fine if more gets done, of course, but the goal for this list is to identify what we're prioritizing. At this point, anything we're targeting for the release should be a top priority.

## Message feed
- New design for PM and stream recipient bars. #23782; includes redesign of focus rectangle and unread marker (currently on CZO)
- UI redesign: Change background color. #23724 (being tweaked / in review)

**Karl Stolley** · 2023-10-05 21:01 UTC

And again, no borders.

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-05 21:14 UTC

Personally I kind of like the borders, but one factor to bear in mind here is that in the Flutter app, we will need a design that does not involve a border.

That's because for an inline span (i.e. for text that's allowed to break between lines as part of wrapping a paragraph), Flutter does not have an equivalent for CSS's `border` property, nor `padding`. Details here:
https://github.com/zulip/zulip-flutter/blob/89fd1f79a/lib/widgets/content.dart#L491-L498
and in the further comments in that function.

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-05 21:20 UTC

There are a lot of areas where the design on mobile naturally diverges from the design on web. But I think the content inside messages is an area where they would ideally be exactly the same design, or have only very subtle differences.

So for that reason, ideally we'd settle on a design to use on web that we're happy with and that doesn't involve a border on the span, so that we can translate the design exactly to Flutter.

(If there's padding on the span, with the padding getting the background color, then that may not translate either. But that difference feels like it'll be a lot more subtle than border vs. no border, even if we do something like tweak the background color to compensate.)

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-05 21:26 UTC

Also in principle the lack of this feature in Flutter is solvable: the code is all open-source, and generally high-quality, so I could spend some time to dive in and write the feature, and should even be able to get it upstream.

But it'd probably be weeks of work, comparable to the scrolling changes I made back in February. (For this feature, the relevant code actually lies outside of Flutter's own codebase entirely, in SkParagraph, which is written in C++ and maintained by a part of the Chrome team.)

So, unlike scrolling, this probably isn't sufficiently core to Zulip's UX to justify that investment.

**Tim Abbott** · 2023-10-05 21:51 UTC

Personally I like the borders on inline code spans, but the Flutter considerations seem like a good reason to do something else. Agree that without the borders we need higher contrast than https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/stream/431-redesign-project/topic/code.20span.20colors/near/1656443.

Yeah, looks like the TextStyle API does offer shadows:

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-05 23:05 UTC

(replying to Vlad Korobov)
> Is it possible to paint a background and enlarge that with some offset?

We can paint a background color, but it will tightly hug a bounding box of the text; we can't enlarge it. That's what I meant above when saying Flutter has no equivalent of CSS `padding` when it comes to inline spans.

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-05 23:07 UTC

You can see a demo of that in the current zulip-flutter alpha — we give code spans a gray background.

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-05 23:21 UTC

Hmm and I thought there was an upstream issue tracking the absence of padding, but I just spent a few minutes looking and didn't find it.

I might have been thinking of this related issue:
https://github.com/flutter/flutter/issues/30541
which is for a knob to adjust how high or low the underline sits when you ask for underlined text. (In particular if we *did* have such a knob, then an underline plus an overline could be used to effectively make a border.)

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-09 19:50 UTC

Yeah, `TextStyle.shadows` produces shadows for the text characters, not a rectangle around the text.

For testing bits of the Flutter API, a convenient tool is dartpad.dev . If you go there and paste in this code (adapted from one of the examples @Anders Kaseorg linked to):
````spoiler demo code
```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const DemoApp());

class DemoApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const DemoApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Text shadows demo')),
        body: Center(
          child: Text('The Turtle',
            style: TextStyle(fontSize: 60, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold, shadows: [
              Shadow(
                color: Colors.black.withOpacity(0.7),
                offset: const Offset(15, 15),
                blurRadius: 5),
            ]),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```
````

and hit the "Run" button at the top, then you see text that looks like this: [image.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/de/XFbW1zOyR1HEIfJeAL_c8hGB/image.png)

which I think answers the question.

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-13 21:10 UTC

I think one thing that's happening with `1px` padding is that it has the visual effect of more padding on the top and bottom than on the sides, which has an uneven look to it.

That's because lots of characters have no descender, or no ascender, or neither, and so the glyph stays well away from the top and bottom of the bounding box, but other than ` ` and `.` and a few others, most characters' glyphs do reach near the left and right edges of the bounding box.

So one reason `1px 2px` looks nicer is that it gives the padding a more even look around all sides.

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-13 21:12 UTC

**Greg Price** · 2023-10-20 04:56 UTC

(replying to Vlad Korobov)
> Code comment on the white bg is still lower than accessible contrast level

Quite possibly it'd be good to darken the color of comments a bit, to improve the contrast.

Even if we do, though, fundamentally there are a lot of different styles in the code highlighting, and it's desirable for them to be distinguishable from each other too, so some of them are going to be fairly light. That means that for good contrast we'll always need to either have the background be very light (when in light mode), or pay a trade-off in reducing the distinctiveness of the highlighting styles.

And as long as the comment color is what it currently is, if we're already below the accessible threshold then it only makes things worse to reduce the contrast further.

MDN: `#f2f1f1` over `#ffffff` light, `#343434` over `#1b1b1b` dark
StackOverflow: `hsl(210,8%,90%)` over `hsl(0,0%,100%)` light, `hsl(210,4%,27%)` over `hsl(210,3%,15%)` dark
GitHub: `rgba(175,184,193,0.2)` over `#ffffff` light, `rgba(110,118,129,0.4)` over `#0d1117` dark
Rust docs: `#F5F5F5` over `white` light, `#2A2A2A` over `#353535` dark

These are all pretty faint compared to what’s currently proposed in #26898. (And by the way, none of them use a border.)

**Tim Abbott** · 2023-10-21 01:05 UTC

I think talking about code is a sufficiently large portion of what our fans do in Zulip that is is worth effort to try to do as well as we can, rather than trying to follow what other products do, though of course other products can be a valuable source of inspiration.

(We also have added constraints, like the multiple backgrounds for messages, that don't apply to these other products and might impact the desired design.)

Personally, I think GitHub's code blocks are OK but not great for having extended conversations about code in inline code blocks -- the bubbles around the code are so huge, and I find the grey backgrounds annoying.

**Vlad Korobov** · 2023-10-21 13:39 UTC

So why not to highlight text color in code spans?

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2023-10-21 18:02 UTC

Are you asking why code spans don’t have syntax highlighting? Because (a) there’s no syntax in Markdown for specifying what language to highlight them for, (b) the way they’re typically used in real messages is for single identifiers or short snippets that don’t benefit from syntax highlighting, and (c) they’re supposed to read as part of the sentence and not grab the eye’s attention away from the text.

## code span colors: dark theme

*2024-06-19 to 2024-07-29 · 35 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/code.20span.20colors.3A.20dark.20theme/with/1830319)*

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-19 01:13 UTC

@Karl Stolley, I'm working on translating dark-theme Pygments styles into the Flutter app (#F749), and I was curious about some of the web CSS as I was reading it. Do you have a moment to think about it? :smile:

I noticed a section of "non-color Zulip customizations", which appeared in zulip/zulip@1c88cc481:

````spoiler "non-color Zulip customizations"
```css
/* Begin non-color Zulip customizations */
.codehilite .c,
.codehilite .cm,
.codehilite .c1,
.codehilite .cs,
.codehilite .ge,
.codehilite .sd {
    font-style: italic;
} /* Comment, Comment.Multiline, Comment.Single, Comment.Special,
     Generic.Emph, Literal.String.Doc */

.codehilite .gh,
.codehilite .gp,
.codehilite .gs,
.codehilite .gu,
.codehilite .kc,
.codehilite .kn,
.codehilite .kr,
.codehilite .nc,
.codehilite .ni,
.codehilite .ne,
.codehilite .nn,
.codehilite .nt,
.codehilite .ow,
.codehilite .se,
.codehilite .si {
    font-weight: bold;
} /* Generic.Heading, Generic.Prompt, Generic.Strong, Generic.Subheading,
     Keyword.Constant, Keyword.Namespace, Keyword.Reserved, Name.Class,
     Name.Entity, Name.Exception, Name.Namespace, Name.Tag, Operator.Word,
     Literal.String.Escape, Literal.String.Interpol */

.codehilite .err {
    border: 1px solid hsl(0deg 100% 50%);
} /* Error */

/* End non-color Zulip customizations. */
```
````

I found what looks like sources for the Pygments Default styles, which web's light theme is based on, and the Pygments Monokai styles, which web's dark theme is based on:

- [Pygments Default](https://github.com/pygments/pygments/blob/f64833d9d/pygments/styles/default.py)
- [Pygments Monokai](https://github.com/pygments/pygments/blob/f64833d9d/pygments/styles/monokai.py)

If my investigation is correct, I think *all* of those "Zulip customizations" are really just parts of the Pygments Default theme, as expressed in that source I linked to. This means that in dark mode, they mix in some parts of Pygments Default so that we don't end up using pure Pygments Monokai.

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-19 01:14 UTC

Here are the parts of Pygments Default that those customizations end up mixing into Pygments Monokai in dark theme:

- italic on: c, cm, c1, cs
- bold on: gh, gu, kc, kn, kr, nc, ni, ne, nn, nt, ow, se, si
- the red border on err

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-19 01:14 UTC

Then I think some other parts also get mixed in for a different reason: dark-theme spans are not only styled from selectors nested under `%dark-theme`, but also from the ones that aren't nested:

- italic on: ch, cpf
- bold on: k, kd

````spoiler (for example)
web/styles/pygments.css
```
.codehilite .k {
    color: #008000;
    font-weight: bold;
} /* Keyword */
/* [...] */
%dark-theme {
    /* [...] */
    .codehilite .k {
        color: #66d9ef;
    } /* Keyword */
    /* [...] */
}
```
````

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-19 01:15 UTC

Is this mixing intended, or do we want to see what pure Pygments Monokai looks like in dark theme?

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-06-20 20:47 UTC

@Alya Abbott sorry, so if the light-mode theme specifies bold on some piece of the highlighted language, but the dark mode theme doesn’t, it will render in dark mode with bold anyway. So to stay 100% faithful to the dark mode theme, we’d have to specify a normal font-weight in the dark mode portion of the file. Does that make better sense?

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-06-20 20:55 UTC

Oh, I see. Can we use pure themes unless there's a specific reason not to? If it's Pygments Default for light theme and Pygments Monokai for dark, that could be fine, but we should have a good reason if we are going to mix parts of themes or something.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2024-06-20 20:55 UTC

Or we can put the default theme directives in `%light-theme`, defined as

```css
@media screen {
    :root:not(.color-scheme-automatic):not(.dark-theme) {
        @extend %light-theme;
    }
}

@media screen and (not (prefers-color-scheme: dark)) {
    :root.color-scheme-automatic {
        @extend %light-theme;
    }
}

@media not screen {
    @extend %light-theme;
}
```

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-06-20 20:56 UTC

Ack, thank you Anders. I was just going to grep for that. That’s a much better solution

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-06-21 16:58 UTC

Updated #30511 with @Anders Kaseorg's suggestions and with screenshots, too.

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-21 18:32 UTC

Yay, thank you!! I see it's merged.

**Notification Bot** · 2024-06-21 18:33 UTC

22 messages were moved here from #**redesign project>code span colors** by @Chris Bobbe.

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-29 01:14 UTC

I noticed another difference from Pygments Monokai, and Greg and I think removing the difference makes an improvement. :smile:

Pygments Monokai gives a default text color of `#f8f8f2`, as the "Token" style: https://github.com/pygments/pygments/blob/f64833d9d/pygments/styles/monokai.py#L32

This style is meant to apply to text nodes that are direct children of the `<code>`. For example, the "context" / untouched lines in a `diff` block:

````spoiler
```diff
diff --git lib/widgets/content.dart lib/widgets/content.dart
index 1715d41e7..43c747232 100644
--- lib/widgets/content.dart
+++ lib/widgets/content.dart
@@ -37,6 +37,7 @@ class ContentTheme extends ThemeExtension<ContentTheme> {
       colorCodeBlockBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.04, 0, 0, 0).toColor(),
       colorDirectMentionBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.2, 240, 0.7, 0.7).toColor(),
       colorGlobalTimeBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(1, 0, 0, 0.93).toColor(),
+      colorGlobalTimeBorder: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(1, 0, 0, 0.8).toColor(),
       colorMathBlockBorder: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.15, 240, 0.8, 0.5).toColor(),
       colorMessageMediaContainerBackground: const Color.fromRGBO(0, 0, 0, 0.03),
       colorThematicBreak: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(1, 0, 0, .87).toColor(),
@@ -61,6 +62,7 @@ class ContentTheme extends ThemeExtension<ContentTheme> {
       colorCodeBlockBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.04, 0, 0, 1).toColor(),
       colorDirectMentionBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.25, 240, 0.52, 0.6).toColor(),
       colorGlobalTimeBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.2, 0, 0, 0).toColor(),
+      colorGlobalTimeBorder: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.4, 0, 0, 0).toColor(),
       colorMathBlockBorder: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(1, 240, 0.4, 0.4).toColor(),
       colorMessageMediaContainerBackground: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(0.03, 0, 0, 1).toColor(),
       colorThematicBreak: const HSLColor.fromAHSL(1, 0, 0, .87).toColor().withOpacity(0.2),
```
````

But we aren't following that. Instead, we use our own color `hsl(0deg 0% 100% / 85%)`, which is the `--color-markdown-code-text` variable.

It makes sense for `--color-markdown-code-text` to be our chosen non-Pygments color in *light* mode, because [Pygments Default](https://github.com/pygments/pygments/blob/f64833d9d/pygments/styles/default.py) doesn't specify the "Token" style, so this text in Pygments Default just inherits the color from its context. (Odd that Pygments lets that happen—but anyway, it does.) But since Pygments Monokai *does* specify this style, it seems like we should follow it.

To confirm this finding, please feel free to play around with Pygments's [demo tool](https://pygments.org/demo/) and see their [style samples](https://pygments.org/styles/) and the [code](https://github.com/pygments/pygments).

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-29 01:18 UTC

Could maybe just change `--color-markdown-code-text` to `#f8f8f2` in dark mode. (Or perhaps there are more invasive changes that would be helpful, toward making it clear which styles are from Pygments and which are our overrides.)

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-29 01:20 UTC

**Chris Bobbe** · 2024-06-29 01:22 UTC

Yes, that's right.

**Greg Price** · 2024-06-29 01:26 UTC

I think another key fact about this fix is that lots of other spans already have that `#f8f8f2` color, when there isn't some more-specific color they're meant to have.

At the Pygments layer that happens because they inherit their style from the base "Token" style. But in the CSS that we use, that's all been denormalized out so that each of those classes like `g` have explicit CSS selectors giving them the `#f8f8f2` text color.

I don't look at code enough to have a personal opinion, but the proposal certainly makes sense barring a clear reason to do something else.

## broader range of font sizes

*2024-12-05 to 2025-03-28 · 207 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/broader.20range.20of.20font.20sizes/with/1993815)*

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-12-05 17:54 UTC

Hey folks! It's getting to be time to push hard on getting the app to work with a broader range of font sizes. Based on the discussion at #**design>information density UI for 10.0** , we ideally want to aim for 12pt to 18pt.

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-12-05 17:56 UTC

The line hight should ideally be adjustable as well, but it's less of a big deal. It would be OK for 10.0 to just offer a dense/compact mode (122%) and a non-compact mode (140%). So we should focus on the font size for now.

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-12-05 18:00 UTC

The core work for this is to go through and make sure everything in the app looks fine at the full range of sizes. @Evy will be taking point on this project, but I expect there will be parts where it would make sense for other folks to help out.

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-12-05 18:02 UTC

Copying some notes from a DM thread, one piece of the project will be to review the results of `git grep font-size: web/styles/ | grep px | grep -v portico | grep -v emails | grep -v bootstrap | grep -v font-size-px`. Those are likely all the remaining pixel-sized `font-size` declarations in the codebase, meaning that those are outside of the info-density regime. They also likely originate from the 14px font-size of compact mode, so em conversions there would *probably* be calculated at 14px/1em. But of course it would be important to verify what elements are being sized, etc.

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-12-05 18:14 UTC

I also did a quick (incomplete) review of what things look like at 20px and 12px, focusing on changes we'll need to make that are not about fonts not adjusting properly. Some things I noted:

1. The width of the right sidebar will need to be adjusted based on the font size. We did this for the left sidebar already, so I think we can do something similar.
2. The width of the settings sidebar will need to be adjusted. We probably also want to let the whole panel get wider for large font sizes, so that notification settings can fit (especially once the sidebar is wider).
[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 09.11.44.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/da/OwDu76KEWcSNs1t1Q2b_gyNT/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-09.11.44.png)
[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 09.13.12.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/f1/AG25YXqSPow2fI2OQp3OGxmB/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-09.13.12.png)
3. The height of the polls modal will need to be adjusted (taller at large font sizes). Perhaps there are others as well, but the modals for moving messages looked all right.
[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 09.10.48.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/92/c8GMeQKZ1mKmF2H71MADc18C/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-09.10.48.png)
4. The "+" button in channel and group settings.
[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 10.07.18.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/7c/VjydYxQCuDooV_fQuZcSSL67/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-10.07.18.png)
5. Message action buttons.
[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 10.08.12.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/d2/qYG1CE6ccFOeVsOzwoRdTLHP/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-10.08.12.png)
6. Empty view placeholder text has weird line breaks at a large font size, and doesn't look to respect the line height setting.

[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 09.10.28.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/db/ADQHCdOXdryH5yOWAN2dzStV/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-09.10.28.png)
[Screenshot 2024-12-05 at 09.29.30.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/18/x4wVIbyqc6qHtQWFGtoJh5Yv/Screenshot-2024-12-05-at-09.29.30.png)

**Evy** · 2024-12-09 23:47 UTC

Hi! Trying to gather context, since I haven't been paying much attention to past work on font size stuff. I'll try to note my questions clearly.

**overall goals**

So previously we had 14px as the default, and now have an option for either 16px (new default) and 14px (compact mode). And now we want to extend support to be as low as 12pt and as high as 18pt? It also sounds like we'd keep the two line heights for now (for new default and compact mode) and use them for the new larger and new smaller font sizes respectively. Let me know if I'm misunderstanding anything here.

:question:  Alya referenced 20px for her (incomplete) review, so double checking if we want to go as high as 20px or just 18pt. 

:question:  Do we want users to be able to set the font anywhere between the lower and upper bound, or will we have fixed points (e.g. 12, 14, 16, 18)? This decision will determine what I'll be testing. 

--

**I'm also seeing there are two main parts of the project:** 

*(1) Looking at parts of the app in the full range of sizes.* 

:question:  Is there a list of parts of the app to look through from the previous info density project? Or should I start one from scratch? I'm imagining keeping this list would be helpful for splitting up the work, each taking areas to work on.

:question: Alya said "focusing on changes we'll need to make that are not about fonts not adjusting properly" -- what's the difference between fonts not adjusting properly and issues that aren't about that? Maybe this will become obvious to me as I start the manual testing.

*(2) Looking for font size declarations that aren't part of the info-density regime*, since they'll be come more obviously different once we have more extreme sizes, and get them integrated into the regime. This makes sense to me.

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-09 23:56 UTC

I think we want to test with 20px; we will likely eventually need to support as high as that for accessibility reasons. I think if we find things that are hard to make work at 20px but easy to make work up to 18px, those are excellent rabbit holes to note and avoid for now. 

But often testing with extra large fonts makes problems extra visible, so it's pretty reasonable to test even above 20px where it feels like it makes your life easier.

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-09 23:58 UTC

I expect we will want to eventually allow all integer font size values in that range. I'm not aware of a common reason that 15px would not work while 14/16 do, so I don't expect that question to materially impact the work, but if you find any situations where intermediate sizes break, we'll want to discuss those (they probably involve bad code).

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-10 00:15 UTC

(replying to Evy)
> Is there a list of parts of the app to look through from the previous info density project?

The main screens where people spend all their time are most important. We can do audits on settings, modals, etc., but most of the work was on stuff like sidebars, compose area, and the main message feed. But as noted previously, often you can find the things without clicking around by grepping for pixel numbers.

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-10 00:15 UTC

(replying to Evy)
> Alya said "focusing on changes we'll need to make that are not about fonts not adjusting properly" -- what's the difference between fonts not adjusting properly and issues that aren't about that?

This is stuff like "This modal that's 350px wide means that at a 20px font size, it always line-wraps and looks kinda ugly as a result".

**Evy** · 2024-12-10 00:20 UTC

And "fonts not adjusting properly" is more like they're overflowing or overlapping other content on the screen?

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-10 00:22 UTC

No, it's something being coded as `18px` which means that item is the same size regardless of `font-size`, rather than being `1.2em` and thus scaling with font-size, so that it visually appears to shrink compared to everything else as font size goes up.

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-10 00:23 UTC

Which is what the `git grep` line in the first message aims to support finding. Most of the ones where it's really bad were caught in the previous sweep; the ones remaining are probably either in corners, or things where it happens to visually look OK at both 14px and 16px sizes despite being a fixed pixel size.

**Evy** · 2024-12-10 00:27 UTC

Oh! I think I see . So, "font not adjusting properly" is referring to the part (2) above, and the screenshots Alya posted are a start on part (1)?

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-10 00:38 UTC

Right, and that `git grep` is a start on part (2).

**Evy** · 2024-12-10 00:42 UTC

```

57
```

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-01-28 17:05 UTC

Not everything we need to change is just a font size, but I think probably 2/3 of the stuff that we're finding through manual testing is in that `git grep` output and could be cleared more quickly starting from the code.

in `settings.css`:

```css
    .input-group {
      margin: 0 0 20px;
    }
```

in `app_components.css`:

```css
    .overlay-messages-container .overlay-message-row {
      padding: 5px 0;
    }
```

```css
    .overlay-messages-container .overlay-message-row .overlay-message-info-box {
      margin-bottom: 10px;
    }
```

```css
    .button.small {
      padding: 6px 10px;
    }
```

```css    
    .button {
      padding: 7px 14px;
    }
```

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-02-14 19:19 UTC

I posted a couple comments in the [tracking doc](https://paper.dropbox.com/doc/info-density-for-10.0--CgXn7H_HRtccBGDis0_S4~jmAg-XVUnZTkKI0EBCXu84o7aV) on the relative priority of some things we've not addressed yet.

**Karl Stolley** · 2025-02-14 19:27 UTC

Great. I'm revisiting the container-query-based stuff for the right sidebar. What I want is to create a small, quick test at UI init time, and if the test passes, the browser gets the container queries. If it fails, it gets the (fallback) media queries.

I think it's better to give a first-rate experience to browsers that can handle it, rather than limiting everyone to the quirks of a browser that cant (ahem, Safari).

**Karl Stolley** · 2025-02-14 19:28 UTC

And as a bonus, once Safari starts shipping spec-aligned containers, users of the updated Safari will get the container queries without our having to do anything at all.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-02-14 20:39 UTC

Yeah, that seems reasonable in general. The main risk is that we will have much less frequent testing on the state with the media query variant. But probably that code will be sufficiently rarely modified that it'll be OK, if we're careful to construct the numbers used in a way that is hooked up to the CSS variables used for the container query model.

## info density: compose banners

*2024-12-11 to 2025-01-04 · 22 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/info.20density.3A.20compose.20banners/with/1999877)*

**Evy** · 2024-12-11 20:35 UTC

```css
.main-view-banner {
    /* ...  */
    /* Banners should carry at least a minimum
       legacy font-size of 15px, and a line-height
       of 18px. 1.2em is 18px at 15px/1em. */
    font-size: max(15px, var(--base-font-size-px));
    line-height: max(18px, 1.2em);
```

Is there a reason we want the minimum font-size? it's more out of place if we go even lower to 12px for the default font size.

**Evy** · 2024-12-11 20:39 UTC

It also looks like the close button was deliberately made to not use `em`, is there a reason for that?

```css
/* Set the font-size for the close button
   on the ::before element, so that it doesn't
   impact the em units above. */
&::before {
    font-size: 16px;
}
```

It looks a little odd, especially above the topic close button which scales with info density settings

[image.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/f4/8bDcnRfy0tbnkxN29ONk0WqI/image.png)

**Evy** · 2024-12-12 02:06 UTC

@Karl Stolley wrote these comments in 520b7b6a9ed425cbae9f2d3348568d6af464db24 -- Karl do you have thoughts?

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-12-12 15:39 UTC

The Inbox was designed at a 15px font-size, so that use of `max()` was originally to keep from having compact mode drop it further. I’m not sure how well Inbox will hold up below 15px, but it’s worth experimenting.

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-12-12 15:43 UTC

I’m guessing the `font-size: 16px` was to maintain a constant size on the close button. I don’t recall a lot of discussion about that, so it was probably a decision to just have one less moving piece in the compose box.

That said, we probably ought to have some kind of UI guidance as to which clickable elements scale and which don’t. Just because someone likes very small text does not at all mean that they like having tiny controls to target.

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-12-12 15:44 UTC

With that in hand, it might be that icon sizes and the like also make use of `max()` to set a lower bound for how small the thing can get.

**Alya Abbott** · 2024-12-12 17:06 UTC

@Vlad Korobov making sure you see this one.

**Evy** · 2024-12-12 19:00 UTC

(replying to Karl Stolley)
> The Inbox was designed at a 15px font-size, so that use of `max()` was originally to keep from having compact mode drop it further. I’m not sure how well Inbox will hold up below 15px, but it’s worth experimenting.

Is Inbox connected to compose banners here in some way I'm missing?

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-12-12 19:03 UTC

It's not, other than that that technique of using `max()` might coincidentally be useful elsewhere

**Evy** · 2024-12-12 21:08 UTC

Would it be okay to switch to not having a minimum font-size and line-height for the compose banner and button for now? If we do want to not scale some things down, that's going to be a bigger project, since most things do scale right now (e.g. the topic clearing x does scale).

**Karl Stolley** · 2024-12-12 21:21 UTC

Probably, yes. The proof is in how things look, of course.

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-12 22:40 UTC

I suspect it doesn't actually make sense for inbox to have a different font size than the rest of the app only in compact mode, and removing that logic would be a good change to simplify everything.

**Tim Abbott** · 2024-12-12 22:41 UTC

(I think we were probably motivated by not making functional changes we didn't have to, in order to simplify getting a basic version of not-compact-mode working).

## scaling of image previews

*2025-03-03 to 2025-03-05 · 23 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/scaling.20of.20image.20previews/with/2110248)*

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 18:45 UTC

How we want to handle scaling of image previews with font size? Apparently, we currently have a fixed number of pixels, so image/video previews will look relatively smaller with a smaller configured font size.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 18:45 UTC

Potentially whatever we do here will be temporary until we do #**design>grey background around image thumbnails**, but that is not planned for 10.0, so we do need a plan for that release.

**Evy** · 2025-03-03 18:49 UTC

This is part of a bigger conversation, but the way we display inline images is probably the part of my experience of Zulip that feels the least polished (the file names showing up by default, the grey background shrinking a lot of images unecessarily, etc) so having them appear a bit smaller at larger font sizes wouldn't significantly change my experience I think. So I don't think it's unreasonable to just leave them at a fixed size for now, unless scaling them up is a trivial change.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 18:51 UTC

I think it's trivial to implement; and I just remembered that we thumbnail to 840x560px quality, so changing 150x100 to some other number should be very low risk there.

**Evy** · 2025-03-03 18:52 UTC

Okay great! Then we can just change the height/width to an em value?

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 18:53 UTC

We just need to preserve the aspect ratio. So 6em x 4em would be the closest approximation with integer values, but probably we want something a bit more thoughtful than that, considering details like how many fit in a row across at maximum width not looking bad, etc.

**Karl Stolley** · 2025-03-03 19:31 UTC

150 x 100 at 14px, since that's the font-size at which that size originated, would be 10.7143em × 7.1429em, I believe.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 19:48 UTC

I propose 12x8 just to use rounder numbers. It'd increase size ~10%, which seems harmless.

**Alya Abbott** · 2025-03-03 20:10 UTC

I'm happy to try that.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 20:16 UTC

OK, someone want to put up a PR for it? I'm not sure if there's other adjacent constants, like the 5px margins, that deserve attention.

**Greg Price** · 2025-03-03 20:25 UTC

That increase in size, and scaling up with font size, sounds great.

Cross-linking a couple of previous threads where we've talked about feedback asking for image previews to be somewhat bigger in general:
#**feedback>Link previews and video embedding@1939087**
#**feedback>Link previews and video embedding@1941732**
and there are several more in #22671.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-03 20:27 UTC

Yeah, we'd planned more serious work on that, but #**design>grey background around image thumbnails** didn't complete this cycle; we should make sure it happens early in the next one.

**Karl Stolley** · 2025-03-03 21:02 UTC

I'll put up a PR

**Karl Stolley** · 2025-03-03 21:10 UTC

#33771 for this. Screenshots:

[thumbnails-before.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/83/eKOLIJCO10XkoS7Tz2ioJcv3/thumbnails-before.png)
[thumbnails-after.png](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/c8/8r6_QoApgC3tbcsbfZo9alOS/thumbnails-after.png)

## Weird bug possibly involving calc

*2025-03-06 to 2025-03-10 · 12 messages · [open in Zulip](https://chat.zulip.org/#narrow/channel/431-redesign-project/topic/Weird.20bug.20possibly.20involving.20calc/with/2113977)*

**Evy** · 2025-03-06 01:12 UTC

Tim [said](https://github.com/zulip/zulip/pull/33813#issuecomment-2701731568) in a PR:

> I do note the top Search UI gets a little weird when you get so many operators that it line-wraps; note the line bleeding through. But that can be its own issue.

I tried and couldn't replicate this, and then realized it might happen if you change the font size and don't refresh for some of the CSS variables to recalculate. I notice this effect in the message feed as well (more space below the navbar specifically).

[20px on fresh page load](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/9c/PgnS3yy0VQ52vjLhp_6_Pn-A/Screen-Shot-2025-03-05-at-17.07.46.png)  vs [20px after changing from 12px](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/2a/3U_i2zDsaPMGG2vWP82Ux51F/Screen-Shot-2025-03-05-at-17.07.16.png)   (mostly look at the space above "VIEWS" and similar top spacing)

[12px on fresh page load](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/7/QOPZ1RKvYxa_Uk8Lmhs8XM_G/Screen-Shot-2025-03-05-at-17.06.29.png) vs [12px after changing from 20px](https://chat.zulip.org/user_uploads/2/a2/9Ebhf-ztrsC43Ml8-clX6d59/Screen-Shot-2025-03-05-at-17.09.34.png)

This hasn't felt like too huge a deal, but I'm realizing now it would have been good to mention it earlier and see how folks feel about it. I'm not sure if there's really an easy way to fix this. I think the `calc` values are cached and don't get recalculated when the `em` values in them change.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-06 01:59 UTC

Ooh, very interesting find; I wonder if that could explain things like #**issues>🎯 issues with topic box height**.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-06 02:01 UTC

If that theory is correct, I think we could have `information_density.ts` recalculate and replace those. Effectively lint-ban `calc` in `app_variables.css`? But we should try to confirm the theory.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-07 19:04 UTC

It seems important to get a handle on whether that is a real issue, and if so, what class of `calc` situations does it apply to. It might be that `calc` is fine, as long as we don't add different unit types when doing so, or something like that.

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-07 19:05 UTC

I certainly don't know how something like `calc(2em + 16px)`  would get resolved, but wouldn't be surprised if the browser did a unit conversion.

At the same time, it would very much surprise me if `calc(2em + 2em)` resulted in anything other than `4em` that scales with font size normally. So there's something we don't understand here.

I'm going to move discussion of that bug to its own topic.

**Notification Bot** · 2025-03-07 19:05 UTC

5 messages were moved here from #**redesign project>broader range of font sizes** by @Tim Abbott.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2025-03-10 21:56 UTC

There’s nothing wrong with `calc(2em + 16px)`. It does not simplify at variable definition time; it’s resolved based on the `font-size` of whatever element it’s eventually applied on.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2025-03-10 21:59 UTC

If there’s some kind of caching bug here, it’s almost certainly something we did to ourselves in our own code.

**Anders Kaseorg** · 2025-03-10 22:04 UTC

I think we’re forgetting to call `resize.resize_page_components`.

**Evy** · 2025-03-10 22:09 UTC

Thanks @Anders Kaseorg! I confirmed that this does fix the issue:

```diff
diff --git a/web/src/information_density.ts b/web/src/information_density.ts
index ee48e245b8..a190209a5c 100644
--- a/web/src/information_density.ts
+++ b/web/src/information_density.ts
@@ -1,5 +1,6 @@
 import $ from "jquery";
 
+import * as resize from "./resize.ts";
 import {stringify_time} from "./timerender.ts";
 import {user_settings} from "./user_settings.ts";
 
@@ -99,6 +100,7 @@ export function set_base_typography_css_variables(): void {
     );
 
     set_vertical_alignment_values(line_height_unitless);
+    resize.resize_page_components()
 }
 
 export function calculate_timestamp_widths(): void {
```

**Evy** · 2025-03-10 22:14 UTC

information_density: Resize page components when changing font size. #33929

**Tim Abbott** · 2025-03-10 22:16 UTC

This story makes so much more sense.

@Sayam Samal  I was looking into #36224 and spent some time exploring the remaining `fa fa-` usages in `web/templates/`. I’d like to work on this issue and wanted to check if this approach makes sense before proceeding.

**Aadyot Nandan S** · 2026-03-29 23:00 UTC

Based on that, I was thinking of splitting the work into a few scoped PRs:
**PR 1 - Settings decorative icons**
Replace static icons in `settings/*.hbs` (info, question, ellipsis, lock, play) - class swaps + selector updates in `settings.css`.

---

**PR 2 - Deactivated user badges**
Replace all `fa-ban` usages across pills, popovers, avatars, and profile modal with `zulip-icon-deactivated-circle` - class swaps + CSS selector updates in `subscriptions.css`.

---

**PR 3 - Stream / message / widget decorative icons**
Replace icons like `fa-user-o`, `fa-bar-chart`, `fa-exclamation-circle`, and widget icons already inside `<button>` elements - class swaps + CSS selector updates + updates where icon classes are referenced in `stream_settings_ui.ts`.

---

**PR 4 - Interactive icons >> icon_button**
Convert `<i role="button">` usages (retry/dismiss in failed message controls, password visibility toggle) to `icon_button` - template changes + toggle logic updates in `common.ts` + related CSS updates.

---

**PR 5 - Overlay navigation (chevron) >> icon_button**
Convert `<div class="fa fa-chevron-left">` in overlays to `icon_button`, updating associated JS handlers in `stream_settings_ui.ts` and `user_group_edit.ts` and CSS.
(Leaving the settings sidebar chevron untouched since that area is currently skipped.)

I’m also leaving the areas the issue mentioned not to touch for this pass (settings left sidebar, bots panel, and `recent_view_filters.hbs`).
